), but we only need Hardy–Krause variation anchored at
\mathbf{0}
for our purpose.
To highlight the special role of
\mathbf{0}
in our Hardy–Krause
variation, we abbreviate it to HK0 variation and use the notation
V_{\text{HK0}}(\cdot)
.

There is a well-known close connection between functions
with finite HK0 variation and the cumulative distribution
functions of finite signed measures. The following
result is a version of this connection, and we will use it for proving Proposition
4.1
. This result follows essentially from
Aistleitner and
Dick 2015
, Theorem 3 with minor modification. We provide our
argument for the modification in Appendix

E.5.1
.

Proposition A.3
.

Suppose a real-valued function
g
on
[0,1]^{m}
has finite
HK0 variation and is coordinate-wise right-continuous on

[0,1]^{m}
. Assume also that
g
is coordinate-wise left-continuous at
each point
x\in[0,1]^{m}\setminus[0,1)^{m}
with respect to all
the
j^{\text{th}}
coordinates where
x_{j}=1
.
Then, there exists a unique finite signed measure
\nu
on
[0,1)^{m}

such that

g(x)=\nu([\mathbf{0},x]\cap[0,1)^{m})\qquad\text{for every $x\in[0,1]^{m}$}.

(38)

Also, if a real-valued function
g
defined on
[0,1]^{m}
is of the
form (
38
) for some finite signed measure

\nu
on
[0,1)^{m}
, then
g
has finite HK0
variation and

|\nu|([0,1)^{m}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\})=V_{\text{HK0}}(g).

Lastly, let us note that when
g
is sufficiently smooth, its Hardy–Krause
variation
V_{\text{HK0}}(g)
can be written as the sum of the
L^{1}
norms of the
mixed partial derivatives of
g
of order 1.
The precise statement is given in the following lemma (proved in
Appendix
E.5.2
).

Lemma A.4
.

Suppose a real-valued function
g
defined on
[0,1]^{m}
is smooth in the sense that
g^{(\alpha)}
exists and is continuous on
[0,1]^{m}
for every
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}
.
Then,
g
has finite HK0 variation and

V_{\text{HK0}}(g)=\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\int_{\bar{T}^{(\alpha)}}|g^{(\alpha)}|.

(39)

B
Results of Section
4
for the case
d=s=2

According to the definition in Section

1
,
\mathcal{F}_{\infty-\text{mars}}^{2,2}
consists of all the functions of the form

\displaystyle f(x_{1},x_{2})=a_{0,0}+\int_{[0,1)}(x_{1}-t_{1})_{+}\,d\nu_{1,0}(t_{1})+\int_{[0,1)}(x_{2}-t_{2})_{+}\,d\nu_{0,1}(t_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad+\int_{[0,1)^{2}}(x_{1}-t_{1})_{+}(x_{2}-t_{2})_{+}\,d\nu_{1,1}(t_{1},t_{2})

for
a_{0,0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and signed measures
\nu_{1,0}
,
\nu_{0,1}
, and
\nu_{1,1}
(on
[0,1),[0,1)
, and
[0,1)^{2}
respectively).
On the other hand, according to the characterization in Proposition

4.1
,
\mathcal{F}_{\infty-\text{mars}}^{2,2}
consists of all the
functions of the form

f(x_{1},x_{2})=a_{0,0}+\int_{0}^{x_{1}}g_{1,0}(t_{1})\,dt_{1}+\int_{0}^{x_{2}}g_{0,1}(t_{2})\,dt_{2}+\int_{[0,x_{1}]\times[0,x_{2}]}g_{1,1}(t_{1},t_{2})\,d(t_{1},t_{2})

(40)

for
a_{0,0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and real-valued functions
g_{1,0},g_{0,1}
, and

g_{1,1}
(on
[0,1],[0,1]
, and
[0,1]^{2}
respectively) which
have finite Hardy–Krause variation and satisfy the one-sided
continuity conditions stated in Proposition

4.1
.
The functions
g_{1,0},g_{0,1}
, and
g_{1,1}
can be considered as particular derivatives of
f
, and they are named as
D^{(1,0)}f
,
D^{(0,1)}f
, and
D^{(1,1)}f
.
Hence, we can write the estimator

\hat{f}_{n,V}^{2,2}
alternatively as

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{n,V}^{2,2}\in\mathop{\rm argmin}_{f}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(y_{i}-f(x^{(i)})\big)^{2}:V_{\text{HK0}}(D^{(1,1)}f)+V(D^{(1,0)}f)+V(D^{(0,1)}f)\leq V\bigg\}.

Also, according to Proposition
4.2
, the functions
g_{1,0},g_{0,1}
, and
g_{1,1}
coincide with another type of derivatives
D_{1,0}f
,
D_{0,1}f
, and
D_{1,1}f
of
f
almost everywhere (with respect to the Lebesgue measure).
For smooth functions
f
, the formula (
35
) for
d=s=2
becomes

\displaystyle V_{\text{mars}}(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\big|f^{(2,2)}(x_{1},x_{2})\big|\,dx_{1}\,dx_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{1}\big|f^{(2,1)}(x_{1},0)\big|\,dx_{1}+\int_{0}^{1}\big|f^{(2,0)}(x_{1},0)\big|\,dx_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{1}\big|f^{(1,2)}(0,x_{2})\big|\,dx_{2}+\int_{0}^{1}\big|f^{(0,2)}(0,x_{2})\big|\,dx_{2}.

This can be viewed as a second-order version of the Hardy–Krause
variation:

\displaystyle V_{\text{HK0}}(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\big|f^{(1,1)}(x_{1},x_{2})\big|\,dx_{1}\,dx_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{1}\big|f^{(1,0)}(x_{1},0)\big|\,dx_{1}+\int_{0}^{1}\big|f^{(0,1)}(0,x_{2})\big|\,dx_{2}.

C
Alternative Optimization Problem for Equally Spaced Lattice Designs

Here we describe an alternative finite-dimensional
optimization problem for solving (
7
) when
s=d
and the design points
x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}
form an equally
spaced lattice in
[0,1]^{d}
.
The finite-dimensional optimization problem in this case can be considered as a discrete analogue of our problem (
7
) for the case
s=d
.
We will compare this discrete analogue with another multivariate trend filtering method proposed in
Ortelli and van de
Geer 2022
.

To motivate the general result, let us first
consider the univariate case
d=s=1
. Let us write the data as

(x^{(i)},y_{i}),i=0,1,\dots,n-1
with
x^{(i)}=i/n
(note that
the index
i
runs from
0
to
n-1
as opposed to
1
to
n
). In
this case, it is well-known that a solution
\hat{f}^{1,1}_{n,V}
to
(
7
) can be obtained by solving the
finite-dimensional optimization problem

\hat{\theta}=\underset{\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}{\mathop{\rm argmin}}\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(y_{i}-\theta_{i})^{2}:n\cdot\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}\big|(D^{(2)}\theta)_{i}\big|\leq V\bigg\}

(41)

where

(D^{(1)}\theta)_{i}:=\theta_{i}-\theta_{i-1},\ i\in[n-1]~\text{ and }~(D^{(2)}\theta)_{i}:=(D^{(1)}\theta)_{i}-(D^{(1)}\theta)_{i-1},\ i\in[2:(n-1)]

and taking

\hat{f}_{n,V}^{1,1}(x):=\hat{\theta}_{0}+n\big(\hat{\theta}_{1}-\hat{\theta}_{0}\big)x+\sum_{l=1}^{n-2}\big(\hat{\theta}_{l+1}-2\hat{\theta}_{l}+\hat{\theta}_{l-1}\big)(nx-l)_{+}.

The connection between (
7
) and
(
41
) can be intuitively seen
by noting that

V_{\text{mars}}(f)=\int_{0}^{1}|f^{\prime\prime}(x)|\,dx\approx n\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}|(D^{(2)}\theta)_{i}|

for
\theta_{i}=f(i/n),i\in[0:(n-1)]
provided that
n
is sufficiently
large and
f
is sufficiently smooth. The second-order difference vector
D^{(2)}\theta
(multiplied by
n^{2}
) therefore plays the role of the second
derivative in the continuous case.

We now turn to the case of general
d=s
where we assume
that the design points
x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}
are given by the
equally spaced lattice

\{x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}\}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\Big\{\frac{i_{k}}{n_{k}}:i_{k}\in[0:(n_{k}-1)]\Big\},

where
n_{k}\geq 2
for all
k\in[d]
. It makes sense then to index
the data with the set

I_{0}:=[0:(n_{1}-1)]\times\dots\times[0:(n_{d}-1)].

(42)

In this setting, our next result (Proposition

C.3
) describes a finite-dimensional
optimization problem for solving (
7
) that is
analogous to (
41
). Recall from
(
35
) that the complexity
V_{\text{mars}}(\cdot)
for smooth functions now involves the
L^{1}
norms of
the mixed partial derivatives of order 2. The finite-dimensional optimization problem in Proposition

C.3
can be viewed as a discrete analogue of (
7
) for the case
s=d
, in which these mixed partial derivatives are
replaced by appropriate discrete differences. Let us first define
these discrete difference operators.

We first define a basic difference operator
D_{d}
for each positive
integer
d
as follows. We use the notation
[p:q]=\{p,p+1,\dots,q\}
for two integers
p\leq q
.

Definition C.1
.

Let
n_{1},\dots,n_{d}
be positive integers and
a_{1},\dots,a_{d}

be nonnegative integers. For an
n_{1}\cdots n_{d}
-dimensional vector
\theta
that is indexed by

i\in[a_{1}:(a_{1}+n_{1}-1)]\times\dots\times[a_{d}:(a_{d}+n_{d}-1)]
,
we define
D_{d}\theta
to be the
(n_{1}-1)\cdots(n_{d}-1)
-dimensional vector that is indexed by
i\in[(a_{1}+1):(a_{1}+n_{1}-1)]\times\dots\times[(a_{d}+1):(a_{d}+n_{d}-1)]
and has entries

(D_{d}\theta)_{i}=\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}}(-1)^{\delta_{1}+\dots+\delta_{d}}\theta_{i-\delta}

for
i\in[(a_{1}+1):(a_{1}+n_{1}-1)]\times\dots\times[(a_{d}+1):(a_{d}+n_{d}-1)]
.

Example

(
d=1
and
d=2
)
.

For
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
indexed by
i\in[a:(a+n-1)]
,
D_{1}\theta
is defined as the
(n-1)
-dimensional vector indexed by
i\in[(a+1):(a+n-1)]
for which

(D_{1}\theta)_{i}=\theta_{i}-\theta_{i-1}

for
i\in[(a+1):(a+n-1)]
.

For
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{1}n_{2}}
indexed by
(i_{1},i_{2})\in[a_{1}:(a_{1}+n_{1}-1)]\times[a_{2}:(a_{2}+n_{2}-1)]
,
D_{2}\theta
is defined as the
(n_{1}-1)(n_{2}-1)
-dimensional vector indexed by
(i_{1},i_{2})\in[(a_{1}+1):(a_{1}+n_{1}-1)]\times[(a_{2}+1):(a_{2}+n_{2}-1)]
for which

(D_{2}\theta)_{i_{1},i_{2}}=\theta_{i_{1},i_{2}}-\theta_{i_{1}-1,i_{2}}-\theta_{i_{1},i_{2}-1}+\theta_{i_{1}-1,i_{2}-1}

for
(i_{1},i_{2})\in[(a_{1}+1):(a_{1}+n_{1}-1)]\times[(a_{2}+1):(a_{2}+n_{2}-1)]
.

Based on the basic difference operators, we define a general
difference operator
D^{(\alpha)}
for each vector

\alpha
with nonnegative integer components.
As in (
9
), we call
D^{(\alpha)}
a (discrete) difference operator of order
\max_{k}\alpha_{k}
.

Definition C.2
.

Let
n_{1},\dots,n_{d}
be positive integers and suppose that

\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{1}\cdots n_{d}}
is indexed by

i\in[a_{1}:(a_{1}+n_{1}-1)]\times\dots\times[a_{d}:(a_{d}+n_{d}-1)]
.
Then, we define
D^{(\alpha)}\theta
in the following recursive way.

•

If
\alpha=\mathbf{0}
, then we define
D^{(\mathbf{0})}\theta=\theta
.

•

If
\alpha_{k}>0
for all
k
, then we recursively define
D^{(\alpha)}\theta=D^{(\alpha-\mathbf{1})}(D_{d}\theta)
.

•

Otherwise, let
\theta^{\prime}
be the lower dimensional slice of
\theta
consisting of
\theta_{i}
where
i_{k}=a_{k}
for all
k\in S_{0}(\alpha):=\{j:\alpha_{j}=0\}
.
Index
\theta^{\prime}
with the set
\prod_{k\not\in S_{0}(\alpha)}[a_{k}:(a_{k}+n_{k}-1)]
.
Next, let
\alpha^{\prime}
be the vector with nonnegative integer components obtained by removing the zero components of
\alpha
.
For example, if
\alpha=(2,0,1,0)
, then
\alpha^{\prime}
will be
(2,1)
.
Then, we can consider
D^{(\alpha^{\prime})}\theta^{\prime}
, which can be defined recursively.
Let
I^{\prime}
be the index set of
D^{(\alpha^{\prime})}\theta^{\prime}
and construct
I
from
I^{\prime}
by adding the singleton
\{a_{k}\}
to the component where
[a_{k}:(a_{k}+n_{k}-1)]
was previously dropped, for every
k\in S_{0}(\alpha)
.
We then define
D^{(\alpha)}\theta
as follows:

–

D^{(\alpha)}\theta
is indexed by
i\in I
.

–

For each
i\in I
,

(D^{(\alpha)}\theta)_{i}=(D^{(\alpha^{\prime})}\theta^{\prime})_{i^{\prime}},

where
i^{\prime}
is obtained from
i
by dropping out the
k^{th}
component for every
k\in S_{0}(\alpha)
.
For instance, if
i=(3,0,1,0)
,
i^{\prime}
will be
(3,1)
.

Example
.

Suppose
\alpha=(2,1)
and
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{1}n_{2}}
is indexed by
(i_{1},i_{2})\in[0:(n_{1}-1)]\times[0:(n_{2}-1)]
.
Since
\alpha_{k}>0
for all
k
,
D^{(\alpha)}\theta=D^{(\alpha-\mathbf{1})}(D_{2}\theta)=D^{(1,0)}(D_{2}\theta)
.
Let
\eta=D_{2}\theta
and note (from Definition
C.1
) that

\eta
is indexed by
(i_{1},i_{2})\in[1:(n_{1}-1)]\times[1:(n_{2}-1)]
.
Next, let
\eta^{\prime}
be the lower dimensional slice of
\eta
, i.e.,

\eta^{\prime}
is indexed by
i_{1}\in[1:(n_{1}-1)]
and
\eta^{\prime}_{i_{1}}=\eta_{i_{1},1}
for
i_{1}\in[1:(n_{1}-1)]
.
Then, since
D^{(1)}\eta^{\prime}=D^{(0)}(D_{1}\eta^{\prime})=D_{1}\eta^{\prime}
is indexed by
i_{1}\in[2:(n_{1}-1)]
,

D^{(1,0)}\eta
is indexed by
(i_{1},i_{2})\in[2:(n_{1}-1)]\times\{1\}
and

(D^{(1,0)}\eta)_{i_{1},1}=(D^{(1)}\eta^{\prime})_{i_{1}}=\eta^{\prime}_{i_{1}}-\eta^{\prime}_{i_{1}-1}=\eta_{i_{1},1}-\eta_{i_{1}-1,1}
for
i_{1}\in[2:(n_{1}-1)]
.
Consequently,
D^{(\alpha)}\theta
is indexed by
(i_{1},i_{2})\in[2:(n_{1}-1)]\times\{1\}
and

\displaystyle(D^{(\alpha)}\theta)_{i_{1},1}

\displaystyle=(D^{(1,0)}\eta)_{i_{1},1}=\eta_{i_{1},1}-\eta_{i_{1}-1,1}=(D_{2}\theta)_{i_{1},1}-(D_{2}\theta)_{i_{1}-1,1}

\displaystyle=(\theta_{i_{1},1}-\theta_{i_{1}-1,1}-\theta_{i_{1},0}+\theta_{i_{1}-1,0})-(\theta_{i_{1}-1,1}-\theta_{i_{1}-2,1}-\theta_{i_{1}-1,0}+\theta_{i_{1}-2,0})

\displaystyle=\theta_{i_{1},1}-\theta_{i_{1},0}-2\theta_{i_{1}-1,1}+2\theta_{i_{1}-1,0}+\theta_{i_{1}-2,1}-\theta_{i_{1}-2,0}

for
i_{1}\in[2:(n_{1}-1)]
.

Let
\theta
be an
n
-dimensional vector indexed with the set
I_{0}
(given by
(
42
)) where
n=n_{1}\cdots n_{d}
. From the definition of the
general difference operators, we can observe that for a
d
-dimensional vector
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d})
with nonnegative integer components,
D^{(\alpha)}\theta
is indexed with the set

I_{0}^{(\alpha)}:=I_{1}^{(\alpha)}\times\dots\times I_{d}^{(\alpha)}
where

\displaystyle I_{k}^{(\alpha)}=\begin{cases}\{\alpha_{k}\}&\mbox{if }\alpha_{k}\neq\max_{j}\alpha_{j}\\
[\max_{j}\alpha_{j}:(n_{k}-1)]&\mbox{if }\alpha_{k}=\max_{j}\alpha_{j}\end{cases}

for
k\in[d]
.
Thus,

I_{0}^{(\alpha)}\cap I_{0}^{(\beta)}\neq\emptyset\quad\mbox{if and only if}\quad\alpha=\beta

for all
\alpha,\beta
with
\max_{j}\alpha_{j}=\max_{j}\beta_{j}
,
and

\biguplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in[0:r]^{d}\\
\max_{j}\alpha_{j}=r\end{subarray}}I_{0}^{(\alpha)}=I_{0}\setminus[0:(r-1)]^{d}.

Here
\biguplus
indicates disjoint union.
Also, we can check that for each
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
,

I_{0}^{(\alpha)}=\{\alpha\}\uplus\biguplus_{\beta\in J_{\alpha}}I_{0}^{(\beta)},

where
J_{\alpha}
is as defined in
(
34
).

We are now ready to state Proposition

C.3
.
We prove this result in Appendix

E.6
.

Proposition C.3
.

Let
\hat{\theta}^{d}_{n,V}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}
be the unique solution to the problem

\hat{\theta}^{d}_{n,V}=\underset{\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}}{\mathop{\rm argmin}}\big\{\|y-\theta\|_{2}^{2}:\theta\in C(V)\big\},

(43)

where

C(V):=\{\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}:V_{2}(\theta)\leq V\}

and

V_{2}(\theta):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\{0,1,2\}^{d}\\
\max_{j}\beta_{j}=2\end{subarray}}\bigg[\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{d}n_{k}^{\beta_{k}-\mathbf{1}\{\beta_{k}=2\}}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i\in I_{0}^{(\beta)}}\big|(D^{(\beta)}\theta)_{i}\big|\bigg].

(44)

Then, a solution to the original optimization problem
(
7
) for the case
s=d
is the function
f

defined on
[0,1]^{d}
by

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})=\big(\hat{\theta}^{d}_{n,V}\big)_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{(\alpha,l)\in\tilde{J}}\big(H^{(2)}\hat{\theta}^{d}_{n,V}\big)_{\tilde{l}+\alpha}\cdot\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}(n_{k}x_{k}-l_{k})_{+},

(45)

where
H^{(2)}\theta
(for
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}
) is the
n
-dimensional vector that is
indexed by
i\in I_{0}
and has components

\displaystyle(H^{(2)}\theta)_{i}=\begin{cases}(D^{(\beta)}\theta)_{i}&\mbox{if }i\in I_{0}^{(\beta)}\mbox{ for some }\beta\in\{0,1,2\}\mbox{ with }\max_{j}\beta_{j}=2\\
(D^{(i)}\theta)_{i}&\mbox{if }i\in\{0,1\}^{d},\end{cases}

\tilde{J}
is the set

\tilde{J}=\bigg\{(\alpha,l):\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\mbox{ and }l\in\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}[0:(n_{k}-2)]\bigg\},

and
\tilde{l}
is the
d
-dimensional vector with

\tilde{l}_{k}=\begin{cases}l_{k}&\mbox{if }k\in S(\alpha)\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Furthermore, for every solution
\hat{f}^{d,d}_{n,V}
to
(
7
), the values of
\hat{f}^{d,d}_{n,V}
at
the design points equal the components of
\hat{\theta}^{d}_{n,V}
, i.e.,

\hat{f}^{d,d}_{n,V}\Big(\Big(\frac{i_{k}}{n_{k}},k\in[d]\Big)\Big)=\big(\hat{\theta}^{d}_{n,V}\big)_{i}\qquad\text{for all $i\in I_{0}$}.

We can consider the finite-dimensional optimization problem (
43
) as a discrete analogue of the original problem (
7
) for the case
s=d
.
Also, the formula (
44
) for
V_{2}(\theta)
can be seen as a
discrete analogue of the formula (
35
) for
V_{\text{mars}}(f)
for
smooth functions
f
. Indeed, for
\theta_{i}=f(i_{1}/n_{1},\dots,i_{d}/n_{d})
for

i\in I_{0}
, we have

\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{d}n_{k}^{\beta_{k}-\mathbf{1}\{\beta_{k}=2\}}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i\in I_{0}^{(\beta)}}\big|(D^{(\beta)}\theta)_{i}\big|\approx\int_{\bar{T}^{(\beta)}}|f^{(\beta)}|

when
f
is smooth and
n_{k},k\in[d]
are large.

Denote by
\theta^{*}
the
n
-dimensional vector with components
\theta^{*}_{i}=f^{*}(i_{1}/n_{1},\dots,i_{d}/n_{d})
,
i\in I_{0}
.
Then, combining Theorem
3.1
and Proposition
C.3
, we can show that the solution
\hat{\theta}^{d}_{n,V}
to the problem (
43
) satisfies that

{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\cdot\lVert\hat{\theta}^{d}_{n,V}-\theta^{*}\rVert_{2}\Big]=O\big(n^{-\frac{4}{5}}(\log n)^{\frac{3(2d-1)}{5}}\big)

(46)

if
V_{2}(\theta^{*})\leq V
.
Here the multiplicative constant underlying
O(\cdot)
depends on
d,\sigma
, and
V
.

Remark C.4
.

The second-order Vitali trend filtering estimator of

Ortelli and van de
Geer 2022
is similar to our estimator

\hat{\theta}^{d}_{n,V}
defined in (
43
).

Ortelli and van de
Geer 2022
studied the estimator that can described in our notation as

\hat{\theta}_{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}}=\underset{\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}}{\mathop{\rm argmin}}\bigg\{\frac{1}{n}\big\lVert(y-\theta)_{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}}\big\rVert_{2}^{2}+2\lambda n\cdot\sum_{i\in I_{0}^{(2,2,\dots,2)}}\big|(D^{(2,2,\dots,2)}\theta)_{i}\big|\bigg\},

(47)

where
\lambda>0
is a tuning parameter and
(y-\theta)_{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}}
denotes the orthogonal projection of

(y-\theta)
onto the orthogonal complement
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}

of

\mathcal{N}_{2}:=\big\{\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}:(D^{(2,2,\dots,2)}\theta)_{i}=0\mbox{ for all }i\in I_{0}^{(2,2,\dots,2)}\big\}.

The optimization problem (
47
) is similar to
(
43
) in the sense that it also aims to find a
solution
\theta
that is close to the observation
y
and has small
second-order differences at the same time. However,
(
47
) measures the closeness to the observation via

(y-\theta)_{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}}
, instead of
y-\theta
as in
(
43
).
Also, (
47
) only penalizes
D^{(2,2,\dots,2)}\theta
, while (
43
) constrains
D^{(\beta)}\theta
for every
\beta\in\{0,1,2\}^{d}
with
\max_{j}\beta_{j}=2
.

The estimator
\hat{\theta}_{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}}
estimates

\theta^{*}_{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}}
but not the whole
\theta^{*}
.
In contrast, our estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n,V}^{d}
effectively estimates

\theta^{*}
.

Ortelli and van de
Geer 2022
provided a theoretical result on the accuracy of
\hat{\theta}_{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}}
(as an estimator of

\theta^{*}_{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}}
) that can be compared to our result (
46
).

Ortelli and van de
Geer 2022
, Theorem 3.2 showed that with appropriately chosen
\lambda
, the estimator

\hat{\theta}_{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}}
(defined in
(
47
)) achieves the rate of convergence

n^{-\frac{H(d)+3}{2H(d)+3}}(\log n)^{\frac{H(d)}{2H(d)+3}}

(48)

for estimating
\theta^{*}_{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\perp}}
.
Here
H(d)
is
the harmonic number defined by
H(d)=1+1/2+\cdots+1/d
.
Note that in (
48
), the exponent of
n

in the rate depends on
d
, and it increases as
d
does.
For example, it is
-4/5
if
d=1
,
-3/4
if
d=2
, and
-29/40
if
d=3
.
On the other hand, the exponent of
n
in the rate in our result (
46
) is
-4/5
regardless of the value of
d
.

D
Brief Descriptions of Datasets

•

Earnings Dataset
.
This dataset is from
ex1029
in the R library
Sleuth3
(see also
Bierens and
Ginther 2001
).
It includes weekly earnings of 25,437 full-time male workers in 1987, along with their years of education and years of experience.
The original dataset also has a few more indicator variables, but we do not include them in our analysis.
We predict the logarithm of weekly earnings from years of education and years of experience.

•

Airfoil Self-Noise Dataset
.
This dataset is available at
https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/291/airfoil+self+noise
(see also
Brooks, Pope and
Marcolini 1989
).
It contains the results of aerodynamic and acoustic tests on (NACA 0012) airfoils of different size, conducted in an anechoic wind tunnel.
The tests measured the scaled sound pressure level of the self-noise of the airfoils at various wind tunnel speeds and angles of attack.
This dataset has 1503 observations and six variables including the scaled sound pressure level.
Here the scaled sound pressure level is predicted from the other five variables describing test settings.

•

Abalone Dataset
.
This dataset is from
abalone
in the R library
AppliedPredictiveModeling
, and it is also available at
https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/1/abalone
.
This dataset is composed of the physical measurements of 4177 abalones.
We aim to predict the number of rings each abalone has from seven physical measurements: length, diameter, height, whole weight, and the weight of meat, gut, and shell.
The number of rings is of interest because it can be used for estimating age.
Here we exclude from our analysis the indicator variable for the sex of each abalone.

•

Concrete Dataset
.
This dataset is originally from
Yeh 1998
and currently available at
https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/165/concrete+compressive+strength
.
It contains the compressive strength of 1030 concrete samples together with their ages and the quantity of seven components, such as cement, fly ash, and water.
In our analysis, we have the compressive strength as a response variable and the other eight variables as explanatory variables.

•

Ozone Dataset
.
This dataset is from
ozone
in the R library
faraway
(see also
Breiman and
Friedman 1985
).
It consists of 330 observations of atmospheric ozone concentration level and other nine meteorological variables, including temperature, wind speed, humidity, and visibility, measured in the Los Angeles Basin in 1976.
Here we predict atmospheric ozone concentration level from the other meteorological variables.

•

Wine Dataset
.
This dataset was first introduced in
Cortez et al. 2009
, and it is currently available at
https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/186/wine+quality
.
It comprises the quality of wines (
vinho verde
) from the Minho region of Portugal and other eleven attributes of wines exhibiting their physicochemical properties; for example, alcohol content, density, pH, and the quantity of citric acid.
The quality of wines was graded on a 10-point scale, with 0 indicating very bad and 10 indicating excellent.
This dataset has two parts: one for red wines and the other for white wines.
There are 1599 red wine data and 4898 white wine data.
In our analysis, we fit models separately for red and white wines, predicting the quality of wines from the physicochemical variables.

E
Proofs

E.1
Uniqueness of Representation
(
3
)

Here we prove the following lemma, which shows that
the constant
a_{\mathbf{0}}
and the signed measures
\nu_{\alpha}
appearing in the
representation (
3
) of
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\infty-\text{mars}}^{d,s}
are
uniquely determined by
f
.

Lemma E.1
.

Suppose
a_{\mathbf{0}}
and
b_{\mathbf{0}}
are real numbers and
\{\nu_{\alpha}\}
and
\{\mu_{\alpha}\}

are collections of finite signed measures as in (
3
).
If
f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}}\equiv f_{b_{\mathbf{0}},\{\mu_{\alpha}\}}
on
[0,1]^{d}
,
then
a_{\mathbf{0}}=b_{\mathbf{0}}
and

\nu_{\alpha}=\mu_{\alpha}
for all

\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
|\alpha|\leq s
.

By the assumption,

\displaystyle a_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\\
|\alpha|\leq s\end{subarray}}\int_{[0,1)^{|\alpha|}}\prod_{j\in S(\alpha)}(x_{j}-t_{j})_{+}\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=b_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\\
|\alpha|\leq s\end{subarray}}\int_{[0,1)^{|\alpha|}}\prod_{j\in S(\alpha)}(x_{j}-t_{j})_{+}\,d\mu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

for all
(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1]^{d}
.
Substituting
x_{1}=\dots=x_{d}=0
, we get
a_{\mathbf{0}}=b_{\mathbf{0}}
.
Suppose for some positive integer
m\leq s
, we have
\nu_{\alpha}=\mu_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
|\alpha|\leq m-1
.
Substituting
x_{m+1}=\dots=x_{d}=0
and eliminating from both sides the components that are already known to be equal, we obtain

\int_{[0,1)^{m}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}(x_{j}-t_{j})_{+}\,d\nu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(t)=\int_{[0,1)^{m}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}(x_{j}-t_{j})_{+}\,d\mu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(t).

(49)

Here
\mathbf{1}_{m}
indicates the
d
-dimensional vector which has 1 in the first
m
components and 0 in the rest.
Note that

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1)^{m}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}(x_{j}-t_{j})_{+}\,d\nu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(t)=\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}[0,x_{j})}\prod_{j=1}^{m}(x_{j}-t_{j})\,d\nu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(t)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}[0,x_{j})}\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}(t_{j},x_{j})}1\,ds\,d\nu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(t)=\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}(0,x_{j})}\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}[0,s_{j})}1\,d\nu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(t)\,ds.

Thus, the equation (
49
) can be written as

\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}(0,x_{j})}\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}[0,s_{j})}\,d\nu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(t)\,ds=\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}(0,x_{j})}\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}[0,s_{j})}\,d\mu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(t)\,ds.

By the Lebesgue differentiation theorem (see, e.g.,
Rudin 1987
, Theorem 7.10), it follows that

\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}[0,x_{j})}\,d\nu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(t)=\int_{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}[0,x_{j})}\,d\mu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(t)

(50)

almost everywhere with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

For a subset
R
of
[0,1)^{m}
, we call it rectangle if it is of the form

R=\prod_{j=1}^{m}I_{j},

where for each
j
,
I_{j}=(u_{j},v_{j}),[u_{j},v_{j}],[u_{j},v_{j})
, or
(u_{j},v_{j}]
for some real numbers
u_{j}
and
v_{j}
.
Now, let
\mathcal{P}
be the collection of all rectangles in
[0,1)^{m}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be the collection of all subsets
E
of
[0,1)^{m}
for which

\nu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(E)=\mu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}(E).

Using the equation (
50
), we can show that
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
.
Also, it is clear that
\mathcal{P}
is a
\pi
-system, and
\mathcal{Q}
is a
\lambda
-system (or Dynkin system) on
[0,1)^{m}
.
Therefore, by Dynkin’s
\pi
-
\lambda
theorem, we have
\sigma(\mathcal{P})\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
.
Since
\sigma(\mathcal{P})
is indeed the collection of all Borel sets in
[0,1)^{m}
, we can conclude that
\nu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}=\mu_{\mathbf{1}_{m}}
.
Consequently, induction argument completes the proof.
∎

E.2
Proofs of Lemmas and Propositions in Section
2

E.2.1
Proof of Lemma
2.1

Although the ideas of our proof of Lemma
2.1
are simple, the proof is somewhat technical because of the intensive handling of indices.
Hence, to deliver the underlying ideas clearly, we first prove the lemma when
d=s=1
and then consider general
d
and
s
.

First, assume that
d=s=1
.
For
l=0,\dots,n_{1}-1
, let
\tilde{\mu}_{l}
be the discrete signed measure on
[0,1]
that is concentrated on
\{u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1}\}
and has weights

\tilde{\mu}_{l}\big(\big\{u^{(1)}_{l}\big\}\big)=\int_{[u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1})}\frac{u_{l+1}^{(1)}-t}{u_{l+1}^{(1)}-u_{l}^{(1)}}\,d\nu_{1}(t)

and

\tilde{\mu}_{l}\big(\big\{u^{(1)}_{l+1}\big\}\big)=\int_{[u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1})}\frac{t-u_{l}^{(1)}}{u_{l+1}^{(1)}-u_{l}^{(1)}}\,d\nu_{1}(t).

Then, for
x\geq u^{(1)}_{l+1}
,

\displaystyle\int_{[u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1})}(x-t)_{+}\,d\nu_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{[u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1})}\big(x-u^{(1)}_{l}\big)\cdot\frac{u^{(1)}_{l+1}-t}{u^{(1)}_{l+1}-u^{(1)}_{l}}+\big(x-u^{(1)}_{l+1}\big)\cdot\frac{t-u^{(1)}_{l}}{u^{(1)}_{l+1}-u^{(1)}_{l}}\,d\nu_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\qquad=\big(x-u^{(1)}_{l}\big)\cdot\tilde{\mu}_{l}\big(\big\{u^{(1)}_{l}\big\}\big)+\big(x-u^{(1)}_{l+1}\big)\cdot\tilde{\mu}_{l}\big(\big\{u^{(1)}_{l+1}\big\}\big)=\int_{[0,1]}(x-t)_{+}\,d\tilde{\mu}_{l}(t).

We also have that

\displaystyle|\tilde{\mu}_{l}|([0,1])=|\tilde{\mu}_{l}|\big(\big\{u^{(1)}_{l}\big\}\big)+|\tilde{\mu}_{l}|\big(\big\{u^{(1)}_{l+1}\big\}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{[u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1})}\frac{u_{l+1}^{(1)}-t}{u_{l+1}^{(1)}-u_{l}^{(1)}}\,d|\nu_{1}|(t)+\int_{[u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1})}\frac{t-u_{l}^{(1)}}{u_{l+1}^{(1)}-u_{l}^{(1)}}\,d|\nu_{1}|(t)=|\nu_{1}|\big(\big[u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1}\big)\big),

and that

|\tilde{\mu}_{0}|((0,1])=|\tilde{\mu}_{0}|\big(\big\{u^{(1)}_{1}\big\}\big)\leq\int_{[0,u^{(1)}_{1})}\frac{t}{u_{1}^{(1)}}\,d|\nu_{1}|(t)\leq|\nu_{1}|\big(\big(0,u^{(1)}_{1}\big)\big).

Let
\tilde{\mu}
be the signed measure on
[0,1]
defined by

\tilde{\mu}=\sum_{l=0}^{n_{1}-1}\tilde{\mu}_{l}

and let
\mu
be the signed measure on
[0,1)
defined by
\mu(E)=\tilde{\mu}(E)
.
Note that
\mu
is a discrete signed measure concentrated on
\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap[0,1)
.
Then, for each
u_{i}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle f_{a_{0},\{\nu_{1}\}}\big(u_{i}^{(1)}\big)

\displaystyle=a_{0}+\int_{[0,1)}\big(u_{i}^{(1)}-t\big)_{+}\,d\nu_{1}(t)=a_{0}+\sum_{l=0}^{n_{1}-1}\int_{[u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1})}\big(u_{i}^{(1)}-t\big)_{+}\,d\nu_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=a_{0}+\sum_{l=0}^{i-1}\int_{[u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1})}\big(u_{i}^{(1)}-t\big)_{+}\,d\nu_{1}(t)=a_{0}+\int_{[0,1]}\big(u_{i}^{(1)}-t\big)_{+}\,d\tilde{\mu}(t)

\displaystyle=a_{0}+\int_{[0,1)}\big(u_{i}^{(1)}-t\big)_{+}\,d\mu(t)=f_{a_{0},\{\mu\}}\big(u_{i}^{(1)}\big),

which means that
f_{a_{0},\{\mu\}}
coincides with
f_{a_{0},\{\nu_{1}\}}
at every point of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
.
Also,

\displaystyle|\mu|((0,1))

\displaystyle\leq|\tilde{\mu}|((0,1])\leq\sum_{l=0}^{n_{1}-1}|\tilde{\mu}_{l}|((0,1])=|\tilde{\mu}_{0}|((0,1])+\sum_{l=1}^{n_{1}-1}|\tilde{\mu}_{l}|([0,1])

\displaystyle\leq|\nu_{1}|\big(\big(0,u^{(1)}_{1}\big)\big)+\sum_{l=1}^{n_{1}-1}|\nu_{1}|\big(\big[u^{(1)}_{l},u^{(1)}_{l+1}\big)\big)=|\nu_{1}|((0,1)),

from which we can conclude that

V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{0},\{\mu\}})\leq V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{0},\{\nu_{1}\}}).

Now, we consider general
d
and
s
.
For
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
|\alpha|\leq s
and
l\in\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}[0:(n_{k}-1)]
, let
\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha,l}
be the discrete signed measure on
[0,1]^{|\alpha|}
that is concentrated on
\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\{u^{(k)}_{l_{k}},u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}\}
and has weights

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha,l}\big(\big\{\big(u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+\delta_{k}},k\in S(\alpha)\big)\big\}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{l}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-t_{k}}{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}\bigg)^{1-\delta_{k}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{t_{k}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}\bigg)^{\delta_{k}}\bigg]\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

for each
\delta=(\delta_{k},k\in S(\alpha))\in\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\{0,1\}
, where
R^{(\alpha)}_{l}:=\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}[u^{(k)}_{l_{k}},u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1})
.
Then, if
x_{k}\geq u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}
for all
k\in S(\alpha)
, it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{l}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}(x_{k}-t_{k})_{+}\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{l}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\bigg[\big(x_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}\big)\cdot\frac{u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}-t_{k}}{u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}}+\big(x_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}\big)\cdot\frac{t_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}}{u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}}\bigg]\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{l}}\sum_{\delta\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}\{0,1\}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\bigg[\big(x_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+\delta_{k}}\big)\cdot\bigg(\frac{u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}-t_{k}}{u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}}\bigg)^{1-\delta_{k}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\cdot\bigg(\frac{t_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}}{u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}}\bigg)^{\delta_{k}}\bigg]\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\delta\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}\{0,1\}}\bigg(\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\big(x_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+\delta_{k}}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{l}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}-t_{k}}{u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}}\bigg)^{1-\delta_{k}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{t_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}}{u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+1}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}}\bigg)^{\delta_{k}}\bigg]\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\delta\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}\{0,1\}}\bigg(\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\big(x_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+\delta_{k}}\big)\bigg)\cdot\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha,l}\big(\big\{\big(u^{(k)}_{l_{k}+\delta_{k}},k\in S(\alpha)\big)\big\}\big)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{[0,1]^{|\alpha|}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}(x_{k}-t_{k})_{+}\,d\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha,l}(t^{(\alpha)}).

Also,

\displaystyle|\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha,l}|\big([0,1]^{|\alpha|}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\delta\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}\{0,1\}}\Bigg|\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{l}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-t_{k}}{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}\bigg)^{1-\delta_{k}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{t_{k}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}\bigg)^{\delta_{k}}\bigg]\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})\Bigg|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{\delta\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}\{0,1\}}\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{l}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-t_{k}}{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}\bigg)^{1-\delta_{k}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{t_{k}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}\bigg)^{\delta_{k}}\bigg]\,d|\nu_{\alpha}|(t^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{l}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\bigg(\frac{t_{k}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}+\frac{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-t_{k}}{u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}}\bigg)\,d|\nu_{\alpha}|(t^{(\alpha)})=|\nu_{\alpha}|\big(R^{(\alpha)}_{l}\big),

and

\displaystyle|\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha,\mathbf{0}}|\big([0,1]^{|\alpha|}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=\sum_{\delta\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}\{0,1\}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\Bigg|\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{\mathbf{0}}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{u_{1}^{(k)}-t_{k}}{u_{1}^{(k)}-0}\bigg)^{1-\delta_{k}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{t_{k}-0}{u_{1}^{(k)}-0}\bigg)^{\delta_{k}}\bigg]\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})\Bigg|

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=\sum_{\delta\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}\{0,1\}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\Bigg|\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{\mathbf{0}}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{u_{1}^{(k)}-t_{k}}{u_{1}^{(k)}-0}\bigg)^{1-\delta_{k}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{t_{k}-0}{u_{1}^{(k)}-0}\bigg)^{\delta_{k}}\bigg]\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})\Bigg|

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\leq|\nu_{\alpha}|\big(R^{(\alpha)}_{\mathbf{0}}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\big).

Now, let
\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha}
be the signed measure on
[0,1]^{|\alpha|}
defined by

\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha}=\sum_{l\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}[0:(n_{k}-1)]}\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha,l}

and let
\mu_{\alpha}
be the signed measure on
[0,1)^{|\alpha|}
defined by

\mu_{\alpha}(E)=\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha}(E).

Note that
\mu_{\alpha}
is a discrete signed measure concentrated on
(\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\mathcal{U}_{k})\cap[0,1)^{|\alpha|}
.
Then, for each
(u_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,u_{i_{d}}^{(d)})\in\prod_{k=1}^{d}\mathcal{U}_{k}
,

\displaystyle f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}}\big(u_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,u_{i_{d}}^{(d)}\big)=a_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\\
|\alpha|\leq s\end{subarray}}\int_{[0,1)^{|\alpha|}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-t_{k}\big)_{+}\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\qquad=a_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\\
|\alpha|\leq s\end{subarray}}\sum_{l\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}[0:(n_{k}-1)]}\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{l}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-t_{k}\big)_{+}\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\qquad=a_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\\
|\alpha|\leq s\end{subarray}}\sum_{l\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}[0:(i_{k}-1)]}\int_{R^{(\alpha)}_{l}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-t_{k}\big)_{+}\,d\nu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\qquad=a_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\\
|\alpha|\leq s\end{subarray}}\int_{[0,1]^{|\alpha|}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-t_{k}\big)_{+}\,d\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\qquad=a_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\\
|\alpha|\leq s\end{subarray}}\int_{[0,1)^{|\alpha|}}\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-t_{k}\big)_{+}\,d\mu_{\alpha}(t^{(\alpha)})=f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\mu_{\alpha}\}}\big(u_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,u_{i_{d}}^{(d)}\big),

which means that
f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\mu_{\alpha}\}}
agrees with
f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}}
at every point of
\prod_{k=1}^{d}\mathcal{U}_{k}
.
Also, for every
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
|\alpha|\leq s
,

\displaystyle|\mu_{\alpha}|\big([0,1)^{|\alpha|}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\big)\leq|\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha}|\big([0,1]^{|\alpha|}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\big)\leq\sum_{l\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}[0:(n_{k}-1)]}|\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha,l}|\big([0,1]^{|\alpha|}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=|\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha,\mathbf{0}}|\big([0,1]^{|\alpha|}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\big)+\sum_{l\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}[0:(n_{k}-1)]\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}|\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha,l}|\big([0,1]^{|\alpha|}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq|\nu_{\alpha}|\big(R^{(\alpha)}_{\mathbf{0}}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\big)+\sum_{l\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}[0:(n_{k}-1)]\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}|\nu_{\alpha}|\big(R^{(\alpha)}_{l}\big)=|\nu_{\alpha}|\big([0,1)^{|\alpha|}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\big).

From this result, we can derive that

V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\mu_{\alpha}\}})\leq V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}}).

∎

E.2.2
Proof of Proposition
2.2

Assume
a_{\mathbf{0}}
is a real number and
\{\nu_{\alpha}\}
is a collection of finite signed measures, where
\nu_{\alpha}
is defined on
[0,1)^{|\alpha|}
for each
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
|\alpha|\leq s
.
Recall that under this assumption,

\displaystyle f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})

\displaystyle\quad\quad=a_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\\
|\alpha|\leq s\end{subarray}}\sum_{l\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}[0:(n_{k}-1)]}\nu_{\alpha}\big(\big\{\big(u^{(k)}_{l_{k}},k\in S(\alpha)\big)\big\}\big)\cdot\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\big(x_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}\big)_{+}

and

V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\\
|\alpha|\leq s\end{subarray}}\sum_{l\in\prod\limits_{k\in S(\alpha)}[0:(n_{k}-1)]\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\Big|\nu_{\alpha}\big(\big\{\big(u^{(k)}_{l_{k}},k\in S(\alpha)\big)\big\}\big)\Big|.

Now, let
\gamma
be the
|J|
-dimensional vector indexed by
(\alpha,l)\in J
for which

\gamma_{\alpha,l}=\nu_{\alpha}\big(\big\{\big(u^{(k)}_{l_{k}},k\in S(\alpha)\big)\big\}\big)

for
(\alpha,l)\in J
.
Then, it follows that

\big(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}}(x^{(i)}),i\in[n]\big)=a_{0}+M\gamma

and

V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,l)\in J\\
l\neq\mathbf{0}\end{subarray}}|\gamma_{\alpha,l}|.

Thus, under the assumption, our estimation problem reduces to the finite-dimensional lasso problem (
13
).
Recall that by Lemma
2.1
, a solution to (
7
) obtained under the assumption is still a solution to the same problem without the assumption.
Therefore, for a solution
(\hat{a}_{\mathbf{0}},\hat{\gamma}^{d,s}_{n,V})
to the problem (
13
), the function
f
on
[0,1]^{d}
of the form

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})=\hat{a}_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{(\alpha,l)\in J}\big(\hat{\gamma}^{d,s}_{n,V}\big)_{\alpha,l}\cdot\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\big(x_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}\big)_{+}

is indeed a solution to the original problem (
7
).
Moreover, since the set

\Bigg\{a_{0}\mathbf{1}+M\gamma:a_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}},\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|J|},\mbox{ and }\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,l)\in J\\
l\neq\mathbf{0}\end{subarray}}|\gamma_{\alpha,l}|\leq V\Bigg\}

is closed and convex, even though there can exist multiple solutions to the problem (
13
), they all should share the same value of
a_{0}\mathbf{1}+M\gamma
, which is indeed the projection of
y
onto the closed convex set.
For these reasons, Lemma
2.1
also implies that for every solution
\hat{f}^{d,s}_{n,V}
to the problem (
7
), the values of
\hat{f}^{d,s}_{n,V}
at the design points should be

\hat{f}^{d,s}_{n,V}(x^{(i)})=\hat{a}_{\mathbf{0}}+\big(M\hat{\gamma}^{d,s}_{n,V}\big)_{i}=\hat{a}_{\mathbf{0}}+\sum_{(\alpha,l)\in J}\big(\hat{\gamma}^{d,s}_{n,V}\big)_{\alpha,l}\cdot\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\big(x^{(i)}_{k}-u^{(k)}_{l_{k}}\big)_{+}

for each
i\in[n]
.
∎

E.3
Proofs of Theorems in Section
3

Throughout the proofs of theorems and the proofs of lemmas therein,
c
and
C
indicate universal constants, which can be different line by line.
Just for convenience, we often use those constants without specifying their values.
When constants depend on some variables, we specify those dependencies by subscripts.

E.3.1
Proof of Theorem
3.1

We exploit the following general result for nonparametric least squares estimators.
This result is a straightforward generalization of
van de Geer 2000
, Theorem 9.1 to the misspecified case, in which the true underlying function may not belong to the function class where estimators are searched over.
Although we only need to consider the well-specified case for Theorem
3.1
, here we state a generalized version for future purpose.
We will use this theorem again for our proof of Theorem
3.4
.

Theorem E.2
.

Suppose that data
(x^{(1)},y_{1}),\dots,(x^{(n)},y_{n})
are generated according to the model

y_{i}=f^{*}(x^{(i)})+\xi_{i}

(51)

where
\xi_{i}
are independent sub-Gaussian errors with mean zero and with a sub-Gaussian parameter
\sigma
, and

f^{*}
is an unknown real-valued regression function defined on
[0,1]^{d}
.
Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a collection of real-valued functions defined on
[0,1]^{d}
and let
f_{0}
be an element of
{\mathcal{F}}
.
Also, let
\hat{f}
be a least squares estimator of
f^{*}
over
{\mathcal{F}}
defined by

\hat{f}\in\mathop{\rm argmin}_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(y_{i}-f(x^{(i)})\big)^{2}\bigg\}.

Assume that there exists a real-valued function
\Psi
defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
such that
t\mapsto\Psi(t)/t^{2}
is monotonically decreasing on
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
and

\Psi(t)\geq\max\bigg\{t,\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\log N\big(\epsilon,B_{{\mathcal{F}}}(t,f_{0},\|\cdot\|_{n}),\|\cdot\|_{n}\big)}d\epsilon\bigg\},

(52)

where
B_{{\mathcal{F}}}(t,f_{0},\|\cdot\|_{n}):=\{f\in{\mathcal{F}}:\|f-f_{0}\|_{n}\leq t\}
and
N(\epsilon,{\mathcal{G}},\|\cdot\|_{n})
is the
\epsilon
-covering number of a function class
{\mathcal{G}}
under the norm
\|\cdot\|_{n}
.
Then, there exist universal positive constants
c
and
C
such that

{\mathbb{P}}(\|\hat{f}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}-\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}>t^{2})\leq C\exp\Big(-\frac{nt^{2}}{C\sigma^{2}}\Big)\qquad\text{for all $t\geq t_{n}$,}

(53)

for every
t_{n}>0
satisfying

\sqrt{n}t_{n}^{2}\geq c\sigma\Psi(t_{n})\ \mbox{ and }\ t_{n}\geq\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}.

Remark E.3
.

The probability bound (
53
) in Theorem
E.2
directly leads to an upper bound of the risk
\mathcal{R}_{F}(\hat{f},f^{*})
.
First, we can rewrite the bound (
53
) as

{\mathbb{P}}(\|\hat{f}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}-\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}>t)\leq C\exp\Big(-\frac{nt}{C\sigma^{2}}\Big)\qquad\text{for all $t\geq t_{n}^{2}$.}

If we integrate both sides of the above inequality from
t_{n}^{2}
to infinity, we obtain that

{\mathbb{E}}[(\|\hat{f}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}-\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}-t_{n}^{2})_{+}]\leq C\int_{t_{n}^{2}}^{\infty}\exp\Big(-\frac{nt}{C\sigma^{2}}\Big)\,dt\leq\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{n}.

Therefore, using the inequality
x\leq(x-y)_{+}+y
, we can derive that

\mathcal{R}_{F}(\hat{f},f^{*})={\mathbb{E}}\|\hat{f}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}\leq\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}+\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{n}+t_{n}^{2}.

(54)

Remark E.4

(Well-specified Case)
.

If
f^{*}
belongs to the function class
{\mathcal{F}}
, then we can choose
f_{0}
as
f^{*}
.
In this case, the condition
t_{n}\geq\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}
is vacuous, and (
54
) becomes

\mathcal{R}_{F}(\hat{f},f^{*})\leq\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{n}+t_{n}^{2}.

We will use this result for our proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Remark E.5
.

Here we briefly discuss how
van de Geer 2000
, Theorem 9.1 can be generalized to the misspecified case.
We describe what needs to be modified in the proof of
van de Geer 2000
, Theorem 9.1 to achieve the generalized result in Theorem
E.2
.

Fix
t\geq t_{n}
and let

{\mathcal{F}}_{j}=\{f\in{\mathcal{F}}:2^{2j-2}t^{2}<\|f-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}-\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}\leq 2^{2j}t^{2}\}

for each positive integer
j
.
It then follows that

{\mathbb{P}}(\|\hat{f}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}-\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}>t^{2})=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{f}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{j}),

(55)

and it suffices to bound
{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{f}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{j})
for each positive integer
j
.
Observe that for every
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{j}
,

\displaystyle 2^{2j}t^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|f-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}-\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}=\|f-f_{0}\|_{n}^{2}+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f-f_{0})(x^{(i)})\cdot(f_{0}-f^{*})(x^{(i)})

\displaystyle\geq\|f-f_{0}\|_{n}^{2}-2\|f-f_{0}\|_{n}\cdot\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n},

and thus,

\|f-f_{0}\|_{n}\leq\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}+\sqrt{\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}+2^{2j}t^{2}}\leq 2\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}+2^{j}t\leq 2^{j+1}t.

Also, since
\hat{f}
is a least squares estimator over
{\mathcal{F}}
and
f_{0}
is an element of
{\mathcal{F}}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(y_{i}-\hat{f}(x^{(i)})\big)^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(y_{i}-f_{0}(x^{(i)})\big)^{2},

from which one can easily derive that

\|\hat{f}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}-\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}(\hat{f}-f_{0})(x^{(i)}).

For these reasons, for each positive integer
j
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{f}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{j}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}(f-f_{0})(x^{(i)})\geq 2^{2j-3}t^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in{\mathcal{F}}\\
\|f-f_{0}\|_{n}\leq 2^{j+1}t\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}(f-f_{0})(x^{(i)})\geq 2^{2j-3}t^{2}\bigg).

From this point, we can simply follow the proof of
van de Geer 2000
, Theorem 9.1.
By repeating their argument, we can show that

{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{f}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{j})\leq 4\exp\Big(-\frac{2^{j}nt^{2}}{C\sigma^{2}}\Big)

for each positive integer
j
.
Combining this result with (
55
), we can conclude that

{\mathbb{P}}(\|\hat{f}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}-\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}>t^{2})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}4\exp\Big(-\frac{2^{j}nt^{2}}{C\sigma^{2}}\Big)\leq C\exp\Big(-\frac{nt^{2}}{C\sigma^{2}}\Big).

Let
{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)
be the collection of all the functions
f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\infty-\text{mars}}^{d,s}
with
V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}})\leq V
where
\nu_{\alpha}
is concentrated on
(\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\mathcal{U}_{k})\cap[0,1)^{|\alpha|}
for each
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
|\alpha|\leq s
.
By Lemma
2.1
, there exist
\hat{f}_{\text{disc}},f^{*}_{\text{disc}}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)
such that

\hat{f}_{\text{disc}}(x^{(i)})=\hat{f}^{d,s}_{n,V}(x^{(i)})\mbox{ and }f^{*}_{\text{disc}}(x^{(i)})=f^{*}(x^{(i)})

for all
i\in[n]
.
Observe that

•

y_{i}=f^{*}(x^{(i)})+\xi_{i}=f^{*}_{\text{disc}}(x^{(i)})+\xi_{i}
for
i\in[n]
, and

•

\mathcal{R}_{F}(\hat{f}^{d,s}_{n,V},f^{*})=\mathcal{R}_{F}(\hat{f}_{\text{disc}},f^{*}_{\text{disc}})
.

Also, we have

\hat{f}_{\text{disc}}\in\mathop{\rm argmin}_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(y_{i}-f(x^{(i)})\big)^{2}\bigg\}.

Thus, once we bound the metric entropy of
B_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)}(t,f^{*}_{\text{disc}},\|\cdot\|_{n})
(under the norm
\|\cdot\|_{n}
) and its integral as in (
52
), we can use Theorem
E.2
(and Remark
E.4
) to obtain an upper bound of the risk
\mathcal{R}_{F}(\hat{f}^{d,s}_{n,V},f^{*})
.

For
V>0
and
t>0
, let

S(V,t)=\big\{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V):\|f\|_{n}\leq t\big\}.

Then, clearly,

B_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)}(t,f^{*}_{\text{disc}},\|\cdot\|_{n})-\{f^{*}_{\text{disc}}\}\subseteq S(2V,t)

because

V_{\text{mars}}(f-f^{*}_{\text{disc}})\leq V_{\text{mars}}(f)+V_{\text{mars}}(f^{*}_{\text{disc}})\leq 2V

for every
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)
.
Here for two function classes
{\mathcal{G}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
,
{\mathcal{G}}_{1}-{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
indicates the class
\{g_{1}-g_{2}:g_{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{1},g_{2}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{2}\}
.
Also, the following lemma, which will be proved in Appendix
E.7.1
, provides an upper bound of the metric entropy of
S(V,t)
(under the norm
\|\cdot\|_{n}
).

Lemma E.6
.

There exists positive constant
C_{\rho,s}
depending on
\rho
and
s
and
C_{\rho,d}
depending on
\rho
and
d
such that

\displaystyle\log N(\epsilon,S(V,t),\|\cdot\|_{n})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{d}\log\Big(2+C_{\rho,s}\cdot\frac{V+\sqrt{n}t}{\epsilon}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\rho,d}\Big(2^{d+1}+1+\frac{2^{d+1}V}{\epsilon}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg[\log\Big(2^{d+1}+1+\frac{2^{d+1}V}{\epsilon}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{4}}

for every
V>0
,
t>0
, and
\epsilon>0
.

By Lemma
E.6
and the inequality
(x+y)^{1/2}\leq x^{1/2}+y^{1/2}
, we can obtain the following upper bound of the square root of the metric entropy of
B_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)}(t,f^{*}_{\text{disc}},\|\cdot\|_{n})
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sqrt{\log N\big(\epsilon,B_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)}(t,f^{*}_{\text{disc}},\|\cdot\|_{n}),\|\cdot\|_{n}\big)}\leq 2^{\frac{d}{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\log\Big(2+C_{\rho,s}\cdot\frac{2V+\sqrt{n}t}{\epsilon}\Big)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+C_{\rho,d}\Big(2^{d+1}+1+\frac{2^{d+2}V}{\epsilon}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg[\log\Big(2^{d+1}+1+\frac{2^{d+2}V}{\epsilon}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}.\end{split}

(56)

To integrate the right-hand side of (
56
), we construct the lemma below.
We omit the proof of (
57
), which is basically the result of simple application of integration by parts, and our proof of (
58
) is presented in Appendix
E.7.2
.

Lemma E.7
.

For every
u>t
,

\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\log\frac{u}{\epsilon}}\,d\epsilon=\frac{t}{2\sqrt{\tau}}(1+2\tau),

(57)

where
\tau=\log(u/t)
.
Also, for every
u>t
and
k>0
,

\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\frac{u}{\epsilon}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}\Big[\log\frac{u}{\epsilon}\Big]^{k}\,d\epsilon\leq C_{k}u^{\frac{1}{4}}t^{\frac{3}{4}}(1+\tau^{k}),

(58)

where
C_{k}
is a constant depending on
k
.

By (
57
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\log\Big(2+C_{\rho,s}\cdot\frac{2V+\sqrt{n}t}{\epsilon}\Big)}\,d\epsilon\leq\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\log\Big(\frac{2t+C_{\rho,s}(2V+\sqrt{n}t)}{\epsilon}\Big)}\,d\epsilon

\displaystyle\qquad\leq Ct\bigg[1+2\log\Big(2+C_{\rho,s}\Big(\sqrt{n}+\frac{2V}{t}\Big)\Big)\bigg]\leq C_{\rho,s}t\log n+Ct\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sqrt{n}t}\Big).

Also, by (
58
) and the inequality
(x+y)^{1/4}\leq x^{1/4}+y^{1/4}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle\Big(2^{d+1}+1+\frac{2^{d+2}V}{\epsilon}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg[\log\Big(2^{d+1}+1+\frac{2^{d+2}V}{\epsilon}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}\,d\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\frac{(2^{d+1}+1)t+2^{d+2}V}{\epsilon}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg[\log\Big(\frac{(2^{d+1}+1)t+2^{d+2}V}{\epsilon}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}\,d\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}\big[(2^{d+1}+1)t+2^{d+2}V\big]^{\frac{1}{4}}t^{\frac{3}{4}}\bigg[1+\Big\{\log\Big(2^{d+1}+1+\frac{2^{d+2}V}{t}\Big)\Big\}^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}\big[\{(2^{d+1}+1)t\}^{\frac{1}{4}}+(2^{d+2}V)^{\frac{1}{4}}\big]t^{\frac{3}{4}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}t\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}+C_{d}V^{\frac{1}{4}}t^{\frac{3}{4}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}.

Combining these results, we can derive that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\log N\big(\epsilon,B_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)}(t,f^{*}_{\text{disc}},\|\cdot\|_{n}),\|\cdot\|_{n}\big)}\,d\epsilon\leq C_{\rho,d}t\log n+C_{d}t\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sqrt{n}t}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+C_{\rho,d}t\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}+C_{\rho,d}V^{\frac{1}{4}}t^{\frac{3}{4}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}.

Now, consider the function
\Psi
defined as the right-hand side of the above inequality, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Psi(t)=C_{\rho,d}t\log n+C_{d}t\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sqrt{n}t}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{\rho,d}t\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}+C_{\rho,d}V^{\frac{1}{4}}t^{\frac{3}{4}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}

for
t>0
.
Then,
t\mapsto\Psi(t)/t^{2}
is monotonically decreasing and

\Psi(t)\geq\max\bigg\{t,\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\log N\big(\epsilon,B_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)}(t,f^{*}_{\text{disc}},\|\cdot\|_{n}),\|\cdot\|_{n}\big)}d\epsilon\bigg\},

by definition.
Also, note that

\displaystyle C_{\rho,d}t\log n\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}t^{2}}{4c\sigma}\mbox{\quad if \;}

\displaystyle t\geq C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma\log n}{\sqrt{n}},

\displaystyle C_{d}t\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sqrt{n}t}\Big)\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}t^{2}}{4c\sigma}\mbox{\quad if \;}

\displaystyle t\geq\max\Big\{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}},C_{d}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sigma}\Big)\Big\},

\displaystyle C_{\rho,d}t\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}t^{2}}{4c\sigma}\mbox{\quad if \;}

\displaystyle t\geq\max\bigg\{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}},C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}\bigg\},

and

\displaystyle C_{\rho,d}V^{\frac{1}{4}}t^{\frac{3}{4}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}t^{2}}{4c\sigma}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\mbox{\quad if \;}t\geq\max\bigg\{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}},C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{\frac{4}{5}}V^{\frac{1}{5}}}{n^{\frac{2}{5}}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{10}}\bigg\}.

Therefore, if we let

\displaystyle t_{n}=\max\bigg\{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}},C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma\log n}{\sqrt{n}},C_{d}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sigma}\Big),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}},C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{\frac{4}{5}}V^{\frac{1}{5}}}{n^{\frac{2}{5}}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{10}}\bigg\},

then the inequality

\sqrt{n}t_{n}^{2}\geq c\sigma\Psi(t_{n})

holds.
By Theorem
E.2
(and Remark
E.4
), it follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{R}_{F}\big(\hat{f}^{d,s}_{n,V},f^{*}\big)\leq\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{n}+t_{n}^{2}\\
&\qquad\leq C_{\rho,d}\Big(\frac{\sigma^{2}V^{\frac{1}{2}}}{n}\Big)^{\frac{4}{5}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{5}}+C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{4}}\\
&\qquad\qquad+C_{d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sigma}\Big)\Big]^{2}+C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}[\log n]^{2}.\end{split}

(59)

We can further observe that the second term and the third term on the right-hand side of (
59
) can be absorbed into the other terms.
Note that there exist some positive constants
C_{s}
and
C
such that

[\log(2+x)]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{4}}\leq x^{\frac{2}{5}}\qquad\text{for $x\geq C_{s}$}

and

[\log(1+2x)]^{2}\leq x^{\frac{2}{5}}\qquad\text{for $x\geq C$}.

Hence, the second term can be bounded by

C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{4}}\leq C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}+C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\Big(\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)^{\frac{2}{5}}=C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}+C_{\rho,d}\Big(\frac{\sigma^{2}V^{\frac{1}{2}}}{n}\Big)^{\frac{4}{5}},

and the third term can be bounded by

\displaystyle C_{d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sigma}\Big)\Big]^{2}\leq C_{d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{2Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\Big]^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\ \leq C_{d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}+C_{d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\Big(\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)^{\frac{2}{5}}=C_{d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}+C_{d}\Big(\frac{\sigma^{2}V^{\frac{1}{2}}}{n}\Big)^{\frac{4}{5}}.

Consequently, removing both terms from (
59
), we can derive that

\mathcal{R}_{F}\big(\hat{f}^{d,s}_{n,V},f^{*}\big)\leq C_{\rho,d}\Big(\frac{\sigma^{2}V^{\frac{1}{2}}}{n}\Big)^{\frac{4}{5}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{5}}+C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}[\log n]^{2}.

∎

E.3.2
Proof of Theorem
3.2

We first briefly recall what Brownian sheet is and introduce integrated Brownian sheet.
An
m
-dimensional Brownian sheet
B_{m}=(B_{m}(t,\cdot):t\in[0,1]^{m})
is a centered Gaussian process on
[0,1]^{m}
whose covariance is given by

{\mathbb{E}}\big[B_{m}(t,\cdot)B_{m}(s,\cdot)\big]=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\min(t_{j},s_{j}).

Let
\{\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}}:k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}([0,1]^{m})
.
Here
\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}}
indicates the
tensor product of the functions
\psi_{k_{1}},\dots,\psi_{k_{m}}
,
which is defined by

(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(x)=\psi_{k_{1}}(x_{1})\cdot\dots\cdot\psi_{k_{m}}(x_{m})

for
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in[0,1]^{m}
.
Then, it is well-known that the Brownian sheet
B_{m}
can be represented by

B_{m}(\cdot,\omega)=\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}\xi_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}(\omega)\cdot\mathcal{T}_{m}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(\cdot),

where
\xi_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}
are independent standard normal random variables,
\mathcal{T}_{m}:L^{2}([0,1]^{m})\rightarrow C([0,1]^{m})
is the
m
-dimensional integral operator defined by

\mathcal{T}_{m}f(t)=\int_{[\mathbf{0},t]}f(x)\,dx\mbox{\quad for }t\in[0,1]^{m},

and the series converges a.s. in
C([0,1]^{m})
(see, e.g.,
Giambartolomei 2016
, Section 5).
An
m
-dimensional integrated Brownian sheet
X_{m}=(X_{m}(t,\cdot):t\in[0,1]^{m})
is then simply defined as

X_{m}(\cdot,\omega)=\mathcal{T}_{m}\big(B_{m}(\cdot,\omega)\big).

Since
\mathcal{T}_{m}
is a continuous linear operator on
C([0,1]^{m})
, it follows that

\displaystyle X_{m}(\cdot,\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{m}\bigg(\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}\xi_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}(\omega)\cdot\mathcal{T}_{m}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(\cdot)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}\xi_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}(\omega)\cdot\mathcal{T}_{m}^{2}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(\cdot)

almost surely.
We will exploit this series expansion of the integrated Brownian sheet
X_{m}
in our proof of Theorem
3.2
.

We also utilize the following two results for our proof of Theorem
3.2
. The first result is

Li and Linde 1999
, Theorem 1.2, which is restated as Theorem

E.8
below. This result implies that upper bounds of
the metric entropy of
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
can be obtained from appropriate
lower bounds on the small ball probability of the integrated Brownian
sheet
X_{m}
. The second result is
Chen and Li 2003
, Theorem 1.2, which is
restated as Theorem
E.9
below. This result helps us
connect the small ball probability of the integrated Brownian sheet
X_{m}
to
that of the Brownian sheet
B_{m}
whose lower bounds are quite well-studied.

Recall that a random element
X
in a Banach space
E
is said to be
centered Gaussian if
\langle A,X\rangle:=A(X)
is normally distributed
and has mean zero for all
A\in E^{*}
, where
E^{*}
is the dual space of
E
. Also, its
covariance operator
\Sigma:E^{*}\rightarrow E
is defined by

\langle A,\Sigma B\rangle=\int_{E}\left<A,x\right>\left<B,x\right>\,d\mu(x)={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left<A,X\right>\left<B,X\right>\right]

for all
A,B\in E^{*}
, where
\mu
is the measure induced on
E
by
X
(see, e.g.,
Lifshits 1995
, Section 8).
Moreover, recall that for the map
\Phi:E^{*}\rightarrow E
defined by the Bochner integral

\Phi(A)=\int_{E}\left<A,x\right>x\,d\mu(x),

the reproducing kernel Hilbert space of
X
in
E
is defined as the completion of
\Phi(E^{*})
with respect to the inner product

\left<\Phi(A),\Phi(B)\right>:=\int_{E}\left<A,x\right>\left<B,x\right>\,d\mu(x)={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left<A,X\right>\left<B,X\right>\right]

(see, e.g.,
van der Vaart and van
Zanten 2008
, Section 2).

Theorem E.8
(
Li and Linde 1999
, Theorem 1.2).

Let
(E,\|\cdot\|)
be a separable Banach space and
X
be a centered Gaussian element in
E
.
Also, let
K_{X}
be the unit ball of the reproducing kernel Hilbert space of
X
in
E
.
For
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
, if there exists some constant
c>0
such that

\log{\mathbb{P}}(\|X\|\leq\epsilon)\geq-c\epsilon^{-\alpha}\Big[\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big]^{\beta}

for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, then there exists some constant
C>0
such that

\log N(\epsilon,K_{X},\|\cdot\|)\leq C\epsilon^{-\frac{2\alpha}{(2+\alpha)}}\Big[\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big]^{\frac{2\beta}{2+\alpha}}

for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.

Theorem E.9
(
Chen and Li 2003
, Theorem 1.2).

Let
(E,\|\cdot\|)
be a separable Banach space and
(H,\|\cdot\|_{H})
be a Hilbert space.
Also, let
Y
be a centered Gaussian element in
H
.
Then, for every linear operator
L:H\rightarrow E
and a centered
Gaussian element
X
in
E
with the covariance operator
LL^{*}
,
where
L^{*}:E^{*}\rightarrow H
is the adjoint operator of
L
,

{\mathbb{P}}(\|LY\|\leq\epsilon)\geq{\mathbb{P}}(\|X\|\leq\lambda\epsilon)\cdot{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\|Y\|_{H}^{2}\Big)\Big]

for all
\lambda>0
and
\epsilon>0
.

In our proof of Theorem
3.2
, we use Theorem
E.8
with
E=C([0,1]^{m})
and
X=X_{m}
and Theorem
E.9
with
E=C([0,1]^{m})
,
H=L^{2}([0,1]^{m})
,
L=\mathcal{T}_{m}
, and
X=Y=B_{m}
.
Observe that the Brownian sheet
B_{m}
is a centered Gaussian element both in
L^{2}([0,1]^{m})
and
C([0,1]^{m})
, and the integrated Brownian sheet
X_{m}
is a centered Gaussian element in
C([0,1]^{m})
.
It is also known that the covariance operator of the Brownian sheet
B_{m}
is
\mathcal{T}_{m}\mathcal{T}_{m}^{*}
(see, e.g.,
Dunker et al. 1999
, Section 3).
Moreover, one can check from the series expansion of
X_{m}
and the theorem below that the reproducing kernel Hilbert space
H_{X_{m}}
of
X_{m}
in
C([0,1]^{m})
and its unit ball
K_{X_{m}}
can be written as

H_{X_{m}}=\bigg\{\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{m}^{2}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(\cdot):(w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}},k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N})\in l^{2}\bigg\}

and

K_{X_{m}}=\bigg\{\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{m}^{2}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(\cdot):(w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}},k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N})\in B_{l^{2}}\bigg\},

where
B_{l_{2}}
is the unit ball in
l^{2}
.

Theorem E.10
(
van der Vaart and van
Zanten 2008
, Theorem 4.2).

Suppose that
\{h_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
is a sequence in a separable Banach space
E
such that if
w\in l^{2}
and

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}w_{k}h_{k}=0,

where the convergence is in
E
, then
w=0
.
Then, for a random element
X
in
E
defined by

X=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\xi_{k}h_{k},

where
\xi_{k}
are independent standard normal random variables, and the series converges a.s. in
E
, the reproducing kernel Hilbert space of
X
in
E
is given by

H_{X}=\bigg\{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}w_{k}h_{k}:w\in l^{2}\bigg\}

with the norm

\Big\|\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}w_{k}h_{k}\Big\|=\|w\|_{2}.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\phi_{k}
be the function on
[0,1]
defined by
\phi_{k}(x)=\psi_{k}(1-x)
for
x\in[0,1]
.
Then, clearly,
\{\phi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\phi_{k_{m}}:k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}\}
is also an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}([0,1]^{m})
.
Also, let

\displaystyle Z=\big\{(z_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}},k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}):z_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}=\langle F,\phi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\phi_{k_{m}}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mbox{ for }k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N},\mbox{
where }F\in{\mathcal{D}}_{m}\big\}.

Note that
Z
is convex and symmetric with respect to the origin, because
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
is convex and symmetric with respect to the zero-function.
Also, for
F\in{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
of the form

F(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\int(x_{1}-t_{1})_{+}\cdots(x_{m}-t_{m})_{+}\,d\nu(t)=\int_{[\mathbf{0},x]}(x_{1}-t_{1})\cdots(x_{m}-t_{m})\,d\nu(t),

where
[\mathbf{0},x]=\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}:0\leq t_{j}\leq x_{j}\mbox{ for all }j\in[m]\}
and
\nu
is a signed measure on
[0,1]^{m}
with variation
|\nu|([0,1]^{m})\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle F,\phi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\phi_{k_{m}}\rangle=\int_{[0,1]^{m}}\int_{[\mathbf{0},x]}(x_{1}-t_{1})\cdots(x_{m}-t_{m})\,d\nu(t)\cdot(\phi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\phi_{k_{m}})(x)\,dx\\
&\qquad=\int_{[0,1]^{m}}\int_{t_{1}}^{1}\dots\int_{t_{m}}^{1}(x_{1}-t_{1})\cdots(x_{m}-t_{m})\cdot\phi_{k_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots\phi_{k_{m}}(x_{m})\,dx_{m}\cdots\,dx_{1}\,d\nu(t)\end{split}

(60)

for
k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
.
By Parseval’s indentity,
Z
is a subset of
l^{2}
and

\log N(\epsilon,{\mathcal{D}}_{m},\|\cdot\|_{2})=\log N(\epsilon,Z,\|\cdot\|_{l^{2}}).

(61)

Also, since
Z
is convex and symmetric, by the fundamental duality theorem of metric entropy (refer to, e.g.,
Artstein et al. 2004
, Theorem 5), there exist universal constants
c,C>0
such that

\log N(\epsilon,Z,\|\cdot\|_{l^{2}})\leq C\log N(c\epsilon,B_{l_{2}},\|\cdot\|_{Z^{\circ}}),

(62)

where
B_{l_{2}}
is the unit ball with respect to the
l^{2}
norm and
\|\cdot\|_{Z^{\circ}}
is the gauge of

Z^{\circ}=\bigg\{(w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}},k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N})\in l^{2}:\sup_{z\in Z}\bigg|\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}z_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}\bigg|\leq 1\bigg\},

i.e.,

\|w\|_{Z^{\circ}}=\inf\{r\geq 0:w\in rZ^{\circ}\}

for
w=(w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}},k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N})\in l^{2}
.
Due to (
60
), we can also write
Z^{\circ}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Z^{\circ}=\bigg\{(w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}},k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N})\in l^{2}:\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}\bigg|\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\cdot\int_{t_{1}}^{1}\dots\int_{t_{m}}^{1}(x_{1}-t_{1})\cdots(x_{m}-t_{m})\phi_{k_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots\phi_{k_{m}}(x_{m})\,dx_{m}\cdots\,dx_{1}\bigg|\leq 1\bigg\}.\end{split}

(63)

We now relate the covering number
N(\epsilon,B_{l_{2}},\|\cdot\|_{Z^{\circ}})
to the small ball probability of the integrated Brownian sheet
X_{m}
.
Recall that we can represent the integrated Brownian sheet
X_{m}
as

X_{m}(\cdot,\omega)=\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}\xi_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}(\omega)\cdot\mathcal{T}_{m}^{2}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(\cdot),

(64)

where the series converges a.s. in
C([0,1]^{m})
.
Observe that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{T}_{m}^{2}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(t)=\int_{[\mathbf{0},t]}\int_{[\mathbf{0},s]}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(x)\,dx\,ds\\
&\qquad=\int_{[\mathbf{0},t]}\int_{[x,t]}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(x)\,ds\,dx\\
&\qquad=\int_{[\mathbf{0},t]}(t_{1}-x_{1})\cdots(t_{m}-x_{m})\psi_{k_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots\psi_{k_{m}}(x_{m})\,dx\\
&\qquad=\int_{s_{1}}^{1}\dots\int_{s_{m}}^{1}(z_{1}-s_{1})\cdots(z_{m}-s_{m})\phi_{k_{1}}(z_{1})\cdots\phi_{k_{m}}(z_{m})\,dz_{m}\cdots\,dz_{1}\end{split}

(65)

for
t\in[0,1]^{m}
, where
[x,t]=\{u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}:x_{i}\leq u_{i}\leq t_{i}\mbox{ for all }i\in[m]\}
and
(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})=(1-t_{1},\dots,1-t_{m})
.
By (
63
) and (
65
),

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}\bigg|\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{m}^{2}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(t)\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad=\sup_{s\in[0,1]^{m}}\bigg|\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\int_{s_{1}}^{1}\dots\int_{s_{m}}^{1}(z_{1}-s_{1})\cdots(z_{m}-s_{m})\phi_{k_{1}}(z_{1})\cdots\phi_{k_{m}}(z_{m})\,dz_{m}\cdots\,dz_{1}\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad=\|w\|_{Z^{\circ}}

for
w=(w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}},k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N})\in l^{2}
.
Since the unit ball
K_{X_{m}}
of the reproducing kernel Hilbert space of
X_{m}
in
C([0,1]^{m})
is

K_{X_{m}}=\bigg\{\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}}w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{m}^{2}(\psi_{k_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\psi_{k_{m}})(\cdot):(w_{k_{1},\dots,k_{m}},k_{1},\dots,k_{m}\in\mathbb{N})\in B_{l^{2}}\bigg\},

it thus follows that

N(\epsilon,K_{X_{m}},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})=N(\epsilon,B_{l^{2}},\|\cdot\|_{Z^{\circ}}).

Hence, for the integrated Brownian sheet
X_{m}
, we can restate Theorem
E.8
as follows:
for
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\log{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|X_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon\Big)\geq-c_{m}\epsilon^{-\alpha}\Big[\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big]^{\beta}\mbox{ for all sufficiently small }\epsilon>0\\
&\;\implies\log N(\epsilon,B_{l_{2}},\|\cdot\|_{Z^{\circ}})\leq C_{m}\epsilon^{-\frac{2\alpha}{(2+\alpha)}}\Big[\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big]^{\frac{2\beta}{2+\alpha}}\mbox{ for all sufficiently small }\epsilon>0.\end{split}

(66)

Combining (
61
), (
62
), and (
66
), we can see that it suffices to find lower bounds of
\log{\mathbb{P}}(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|X_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon)
in order to find upper bounds of the metric entropy of
{\mathcal{D}}_{m}
.

We now turn to the small ball probability of
X_{m}
.
Recall that the covariance operator of the Brownian sheet
B_{m}
is
\mathcal{T}_{m}\mathcal{T}_{m}^{*}
.
By applying Theorem
E.9
to
E=C([0,1]^{m})
,
H=L^{2}([0,1]^{m})
,
L=\mathcal{T}_{m}
, and
X=Y=B_{m}
, we can derive that

{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|X_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon\Big)\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|B_{m}(t)|\leq\lambda\epsilon\Big)\cdot{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\int_{[0,1]^{m}}B_{m}(t)^{2}\,dt\Big)\Big].

for all
\lambda>0
and
\epsilon>0
.
Substituting
\lambda=\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{3}}
and using Markov’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|X_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon\Big)\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|B_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}\Big)\cdot{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\exp\Big(-\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{4}{3}}}{2}\int_{[0,1]^{m}}B_{m}(t)^{2}\,dt\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\geq{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|B_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}\Big)\cdot\Big[{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|B_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(-{\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{3}}}{2}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\Big[{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|B_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}\Big)\Big]^{2}\cdot\exp\Big(-{\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{3}}}{2}}\Big).

In
Dunker et al. 1999
, Theorem 6, it was proved that

\log{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|B_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon\Big)\geq-c_{m}\epsilon^{-2}\Big[\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big]^{2m-1}

for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.
In particular, it is well-known that the logarithmic multiplicative factor of the right-hand side can be omitted when
m=1
.
Therefore, it follows that

\displaystyle\log{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|X_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon\Big)\geq 2\log{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]^{m}}|B_{m}(t)|\leq\epsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}\Big)-{\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{3}}}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq-c_{m}\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{3}}\Big[\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big]^{2m-1}-{\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{3}}}{2}}\geq-c_{m}\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{3}}\Big[\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big]^{2m-1}

for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.
This result together with (
66
) implies that

\log N(\epsilon,B_{l_{2}},\|\cdot\|_{Z^{\circ}})\leq C_{m}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big[\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big]^{\frac{3(2m-1)}{4}}

for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.
Consequently, combining all the pieces, we can prove that

\log N(\epsilon,{\mathcal{D}}_{m},\|\cdot\|_{2})\leq C_{m}\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big[\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big]^{\frac{3(2m-1)}{4}}

for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.
Note that we can omit the logarithmic factor when
m=1
.
∎

E.3.3
Proof of Theorem
3.4

Our proof of Theorem
3.4
uses the following lemma, which we prove in Appendix
E.7.3
.
This lemma states that for every function
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\infty-\text{mars}}^{d,s}
, we can find
f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\mu_{\alpha}\}}
which is close to
f
and whose
\mu_{\alpha}
are discrete signed measures concentrated on the lattices generated by
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{k}
.
We will use this lemma again for our proof of Theorem
3.8
.

Lemma E.11
.

Suppose we are given a real number
a_{\mathbf{0}}
and a collection of finite signed measures
\{\nu_{\alpha}\}
where
\nu_{\alpha}
is defined on
[0,1)^{|\alpha|}
for each
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
|\alpha|\leq s
.
Then, there exists a collection of discrete signed measures
\{\mu_{\alpha}\}
where
\mu_{\alpha}
is concentrated on
(\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{k})\cap[0,1)^{|\alpha|}
for each
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
|\alpha|\leq s
such that

(i)

V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\mu_{\alpha}\}})\leq V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}})
,

(ii)

\|f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\mu_{\alpha}\}}-f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}}\|_{\infty}\leq(2/N)\cdot V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}})
, and

(iii)

\|f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\mu_{\alpha}\}}-f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}}\|_{p_{0},2}\leq C_{d}(B/N^{3})^{1/2}\cdot V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}})

for some positive constant
C_{d}
depending on
d
, where
N=\min_{k}N_{k}
.

Recall the definition of
{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)
from our proof of Theorem
3.1
.
Also, let
{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{approx}}(V)
be the collection of all the functions
f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\infty-\text{mars}}^{d,s}
with
V_{\text{mars}}(f_{a_{\mathbf{0}},\{\nu_{\alpha}\}})\leq V
where
\nu_{\alpha}
is concentrated on
(\prod_{k\in S(\alpha)}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{k})\cap[0,1)^{|\alpha|}
for each
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
|\alpha|\leq s
.
Then, clearly,

\tilde{f}^{d,s}_{n,V}\in\mathop{\rm argmin}_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{approx}}(V)}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(y_{i}-f(x^{(i)})\big)^{2}\bigg\}.

Also, Lemma
E.11
guarantees that we have
f_{0}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{approx}}(V)
for which

\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}\leq\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2V}{N}.

In order to apply Theorem
E.2
with
f_{0}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{approx}}(V)
, we need to bound the metric entropy of
B_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{approx}}(V)}(t,f_{0},\|\cdot\|_{n})
(under the norm
\|\cdot\|_{n}
).
By Lemma
2.1
, there exists
g_{0}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)
such that

g_{0}(x^{(i)})=f_{0}(x^{(i)})

for all
i\in[n]
.
Also, by the same lemma and the fact that the norm
\|\cdot\|_{n}
only depends on the values of functions at
x^{(i)}
, we have

N\big(\epsilon,B_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{approx}}(V)}(t,f_{0},\|\cdot\|_{n}\big),\|\cdot\|_{n})\leq N\big(\epsilon,B_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)}(t,g_{0},\|\cdot\|_{n}),\|\cdot\|_{n}\big)

for every
\epsilon>0
.
Hence, we can repeat our argument in the proof of Theorem
3.1
to bound
N(\epsilon,B_{{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{disc}}(V)}(t,g_{0},\|\cdot\|_{n}),\|\cdot\|_{n})
and thereby show that the function
\Psi
defined by

\displaystyle\Psi(t)=C_{\rho,d}t\log n+C_{d}t\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sqrt{n}t}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{\rho,d}t\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}+C_{\rho,d}V^{\frac{1}{4}}t^{\frac{3}{4}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}}

for
t>0
and

\displaystyle t_{n}=\max\bigg\{\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}},C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma\log n}{\sqrt{n}},C_{d}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sigma}\Big),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{8}},C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{\frac{4}{5}}V^{\frac{1}{5}}}{n^{\frac{2}{5}}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{10}}\bigg\},

satisfy the conditions in Theorem
E.2
.
As a result, Theorem
E.2
(and Remark
E.3
) implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{F}\big(\tilde{f}^{d,s}_{n,V},f^{*}\big)\leq\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{n}^{2}+\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{n}+t_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{8V^{2}}{N^{2}}+C_{\rho,d}\Big(\frac{\sigma^{2}V^{\frac{1}{2}}}{n}\Big)^{\frac{4}{5}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{5}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{4}}+C_{d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{2V}{\sigma}\Big)\Big]^{2}+C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}[\log n]^{2}.

As in the proof of Theorem
3.1
, we can absorb the third and the fourth term into the other terms.
Consequently, we can derive that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{F}\big(\tilde{f}^{d,s}_{n,V},f^{*}\big)\leq\frac{8V^{2}}{N^{2}}+C_{\rho,d}\Big(\frac{\sigma^{2}V^{\frac{1}{2}}}{n}\Big)^{\frac{4}{5}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{Vn^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sigma}\Big)\bigg]^{\frac{3(2s-1)}{5}}+C_{\rho,d}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}[\log n]^{2}

as desired.
∎

E.3.4
Proof of Theorem
3.5

The following theorem plays a central role in our proof of Theorem
3.5
.
This theorem is a simple extension of
Han and Wellner 2019
, Proposition 2 to the misspecified case, where estimators are searched over the function class that may not contain the true underlying function.
For Theorem
3.5
, we only need the partial result of Theorem
E.12
concerning the well-specified case.
The more general result covering the misspecified case is only required for Theorem
3.8
.
However, here we state the full result in order to avoid redundancy.
We provide our proof of Theorem
E.12
in Appendix
E.7.4
.

Theorem E.12
.

Suppose that data
(X^{(1)},y_{1}),\dots,(X^{(n)},y_{n})
are generated according to the model

y_{i}=f^{*}(X^{(i)})+\xi_{i},

(67)

where
X^{(i)}
are i.i.d. random variables with law
P
on
\mathcal{X}
,
\xi_{i}
are i.i.d. errors independent of
X^{(1)},\dots,X^{(n)}
with mean zero and with finite
L^{q}
norm for some
q\geq 3
, and

f^{*}
is an unknown real-valued regression function defined on
\mathcal{X}
.
Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a countable collection of real-valued functions defined on
\mathcal{X}
and
f_{0}
be an element of
{\mathcal{F}}
, and assume that
\|f-f_{0}\|_{\infty}\leq M
for every
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
.
Also, let
\hat{f}
be an estimator of
f^{*}
for which

\hat{f}\in\mathop{\rm argmin}_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(y_{i}-f(X^{(i)})\big)^{2}\bigg\}

with probability at least
1-\epsilon
for some
\epsilon\geq 0
.
Moreover, suppose that there exists
t_{n}\geq 4\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{P,2}
such that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in{\mathcal{F}}-\{f_{0}\}\\
\|f\|_{P,2}\leq rt_{n}\end{subarray}}\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}f(X^{(i)})\Big|\bigg]\leq r\sqrt{n}t_{n}^{2},

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in{\mathcal{F}}-\{f_{0}\}\\
\|f\|_{P,2}\leq rt_{n}\end{subarray}}\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(X^{(i)})\Big|\bigg]\leq r\sqrt{n}t_{n}^{2},

and

{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in{\mathcal{F}}-\{f_{0}\}\\
\|f\|_{P,2}\leq rt_{n}\end{subarray}}\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}f(X^{(i)})\cdot(f_{0}-f^{*})(X^{(i)})\Big|\bigg]\leq r\sqrt{n}t_{n}^{2}

for every
r\geq 1
.
Here
\|\cdot\|_{P,2}
is the norm defined by

\|f\|_{P,2}=\big({\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P}[f(X)^{2}]\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

and
\epsilon_{i}
are i.i.d. Rademacher variables independent of
X^{(1)},\dots,X^{(n)}
.
Then, there exists a universal positive constant
C
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathbb{P}}(\|\hat{f}-f_{0}\|_{P,2}>t)\leq\epsilon&+C\cdot\frac{1+M^{3}}{t^{3}}\cdot t_{n}^{3}+C\cdot\frac{\|\xi_{1}\|_{2}^{3}+\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{\infty}^{3}+M^{3}}{n^{\frac{3}{2}}t^{3}}\\
&+C\cdot\frac{M^{3}(\|\xi_{1}\|_{q}^{3}n^{\frac{3}{q}}+\|f_{0}-f^{*}\|_{\infty}^{3}+M^{3})}{n^{3}t^{6}}\end{split}

(68)

for every
t\geq t_{n}
.

Remark E.13
.

The reason why
{\mathcal{F}}
is assumed to be countable in Theorem
E.12
is to guarantee the measurability of the supremums.
We can remove this condition when
{\mathcal{F}}
is a subset of
C([0,1])^{m}
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{F}}
is a subset of
C([0,1])^{m}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
is a subset of
